        The conflict arose because:
            •	The Dutch republic of Transvaal refused to grant political rights to Anglo-Saxon immigrants.
            •	Other Boer republics also denied these rights.
        In Britain, public opinion saw this as a just war. The Boers were denying lawful rights to British citizens.
            However, European and global opinion viewed it as British imperialism.

    The Boer War
        At the start of the 20th century, Britain and the Boers clashed over South Africa. 
            The Boers, having previously defeated Britain, were aware of its military weaknesses in the region. 
            They believed that before the war ended, some European power would intervene on their behalf.
        British Underestimation
            The British underestimated:
                •	The tenacity and tactical skill of Boer fighters.
                •	The advantage of fighting on home ground.
            The Dutch-class irregulars were strong fighters but suffered from:
                •	Lax discipline
                •	Excessive caution
                •	Lack of unified strategy
            Had they overcome these weaknesses, the war could have lasted longer in their favor.
            Course of the War
                •	Several small battles took place.
                •	Boer forces used thin, extended defensive lines, making them difficult to penetrate.
                •	The British gradually gained successes.
            The war spanned thousands of square miles and involved 450,000 British soldiers. It ended on 31 May 1902, after a two-and-a-half-year campaign.
            The British secured victory by:
                •	Splitting the war zone into smaller resistance areas.
                •	Using mounted columns to strike at Boer mobility.
            Impact of the War
                Inside Britain:
                    •	The Liberals came to power in 1905.
                    •	The new Secretary of State for War, R.B. Haldane, took key steps:
                    •	Maintained British naval supremacy.
                    •	Reorganized the army, drawing lessons from the Boer War.
                Outside Britain:
                    •	The war showcased British imperial unity.
                    •	Britain’s military reputation suffered due to prolonged Boer resistance.
                    •	However, Britain’s naval power prevented European rivals from aiding the Boers.

    - Politics of Alliances
        At the beginning of the 20th century, Britain’s long-standing policy of splendid isolation was becoming impractical. 
            Maintaining naval supremacy alone was no longer sufficient, and Britain began seeking an ally on the European mainland.
        3.1. Germany as a Potential Ally
            Britain initially considered Germany due to:
                •	Its military strength, as the most formidable power in Europe.
                •	Its rising population and tradition of aggressive diplomacy.
                •	Its potential as a counterweight to Russia, Britain’s key rival in the Middle and Far East.
                •	Its historical ties with Britain:
                    •	The Kaiser of Germany was Queen Victoria’s grandson.
                    •	Germany had helped Wellington at Waterloo.
            British Efforts to Secure an Alliance
                •	During the Boer War, British Colonial Secretary Joseph Chamberlain sought closer ties with Germany.
                •	He proposed a secret agreement over Morocco, where Germany’s influence was growing.
            German Resistance
                •	Baron von Holstein, a German Foreign Ministry official, advised the Kaiser against an alliance.
                •	He argued that Britain always used others for its own benefit.
                •	He encouraged Germany to demand a higher price for its friendship.
                •	Holstein believed a British-French alliance was unlikely, making Britain dependent on Germany.
        3.2. Shift Towards France
            Britain, frustrated by Germany’s reluctance, began reconsidering its alliances.
            Factors Leading to a French Alliance
                •	The British press urged an understanding with Russia, even at the cost of concessions in China and the Persian Gulf.
                •	Growing concerns over Germany’s expanding naval power pushed Britain to seek alternatives.
                •	France, though a traditional enemy, became a potential partner.
            Challenges in a British-French Alliance
                •	Britain was aristocratic, whereas France was the only acknowledged republic in Europe.
                •	France had suffered British indifference when defeated by Germany thirty years earlier.
                •	The two nations had nearly gone to war in 1898 over Fashoda (later Kodok) in Sudan.
                •	France’s internal instability:
                    •	It was seen as divided, with a weak government.
                    •	The appointment of Alexander Millerand, a Socialist, to the French Cabinet in 1899 worried Britain’s Conservative government.

    - The Anglo-French Entente
        Despite obstacles, Anglo-French relations improved at the start of the 20th century.
        4.1. The Role of King Edward VII
            •	May 1903: King Edward VII visited Paris, breaking the diplomatic deadlock.
            •	His visit paved the way for negotiations between:
                •	Lansdowne (British Foreign Minister)
                •	Delcassé (French Foreign Minister)
        4.2. Formation of the Entente (April 1904)
            The negotiations resulted in a series of agreements known as the Anglo-French Entente, the most significant diplomatic event of the early 20th century.
                It remained in effect until France fell in World War II.
        4.3. Key Agreements
            4.3.1. First Agreement (Egypt & Morocco)
                •	Britain agreed not to alter Egypt’s political status.
                    •	Egypt remained a semi-independent Turkish province but was under British occupation.
                    •	France would not interfere in British affairs there.
                •	France agreed not to alter Morocco’s political status.
                    •	Morocco was under French influence.
                    •	Britain would not interfere in French affairs there.
                •	Additional terms:
                    •	Trade freedom in Egypt and Morocco for 30 years.
                    •	No fortifications on the Moorish coast opposite Gibraltar.
                    •	France would negotiate separately with Spain on Morocco’s status.
                    •	The Egyptian government could manage its finances, provided it paid its debt interest.
                    •	Suez Canal matters were settled as per French wishes.
            4.3.2. Second Agreement (Newfoundland & West Africa)
                •	France gave up its fishing rights off Newfoundland’s coast.
                •	In return, France gained uninterrupted access between its territories on:
                    •	The Niger River.
                    •	Lake Chad (West Africa).
            4.3.3. Third Agreement (Southeast Asia & Madagascar)
                •	Both nations agreed to:
                    •	No military intervention or special privileges in the Menam Basin (Thailand).
                •	France recognized British influence:
                    •	West of the neutral zone (Siamese territory).
                    •	Gulf of Siam, Malaya Peninsula, and adjacent islands.
                •	Britain recognized French influence:
                    •	East and southeast of the neutral zone.
                •	Britain withdrew objections to:
                    •	French tariffs in Madagascar (occupied by France in 1896).
                •	1906: Britain and France established a joint rule (condominium) over the New Hebrides islands.
        4.4. Impact of the Anglo-French Entente
            •	The agreement resolved many outstanding disputes between Britain and France.
            •	It strengthened their diplomatic ties, paving the way for future cooperation.
            •	However, it also deepened European divisions, leading to the formation of two opposing military blocs.
            •	Lord Rosebery (British ex-Premier) noted:
                •	Germany, Europe’s strongest military power, was excluded from the negotiations.
                •	The neglect of Germany’s interests in Morocco contributed to growing tensions.
                •	Ultimately, this pushed Britain towards war against Germany.

    - The Franco-Russian Alliance
        The Anglo-French Entente influenced Russo-British relations, which had been hostile for most of the 19th century.
        5.1. Russia’s Shift in Focus
            •	After bitter experiences in Bulgaria, the Russian Tsars turned their attention towards the Far East.
            •	The collapsing Chinese Empire presented new expansion opportunities for Russia.
            •	To secure Western support, Russia sought an alliance with France.
        5.2. Strengthening Franco-Russian Ties
            •	Since the late 1880s, relations between France and Russia improved due to:
                •	Financial transactions.
                •	Military exchanges.
            •	1891: A political agreement was reached to coordinate foreign policy.
            •	1895: A military convention was officially announced.
                •	The alliance aimed to counter any attack by the Triple Alliance (Germany, Austria-Hungary, Italy).
        5.3. Reaction from Other Powers
            •	The Franco-Russian Alliance was met with suspicion and disapproval in Berlin and London.
            •	However, the alliance did not mask the military and political weaknesses of both partners.
            •	Historians note:
                •	Both France and Russia were weaker than they appeared.
                •	Over the next ten years, their weaknesses became evident.
                •	Only when Britain later joined the alliance did it become a true counterbalance to the Triple Alliance.

    - The Status of the Triple Alliance
        The Triple Alliance consisted of Germany, Austria-Hungary, and Italy, but internal tensions and conflicting interests weakened its unity.
        6.1. Austria-Hungary: A Fragile Partner
            •	Apart from Germany, the most significant power in the alliance was Austria-Hungary.
            •	As a multiethnic empire, it faced challenges from rising nationalism.
            •	Emperor Franz Josef followed a moderate policy:
                •	He refused to impose military dictatorship in Bohemia.
                •	However, Germany and Hungary formed an unholy alliance to undermine the empire’s balance.
            •	For Germany, Austria-Hungary was merely:
                •	A recruitment ground for its army.
                •	A buffer state against Russian expansion.
            •	Austria-Hungary adopted anti-Slav policies to align with German interests, despite many Slavs being loyal subjects.
            •	This approach also suited Hungary, which had its own record of mistreating minorities.
        6.2. Italy: A Reluctant Ally
            •	Italy joined the Triple Alliance despite its historical enmity with Austria-Hungary.
            •	Its decision was driven by hostility towards France due to:
                •	France’s imperialist policies in Africa.
                •	Mediterranean territorial disputes.
            •	Italy resented French control over:
                •	Savoy
                •	Nice
                •	Corsica
            •	Though a formal member, Italy’s commitment to the alliance remained weak, as its national interests often clashed with those of its allies.

    - The Status of the Ottoman Empire
        The Ottoman Empire sprawled across the Balkans, the Near East, and North Africa. Despite its declining power, it remained a force capable of maintaining its vast, multiethnic dominions.
        7.1. The “Sick Man of Europe”
            •	Tsar Nicholas I famously referred to the Ottoman Empire as the “Sick Man of Europe”, highlighting its weakening position.
            •	However, the empire still managed to hold its diverse territories together, despite internal and external challenges.
        7.2. Military Strength and Resilience
            •	In the late 19th century, the Ottomans proved their resilience by:
            •	Crushing an Armenian revolt.
            •	Defeating Greece decisively in battle.
        •	These victories demonstrated that, although in decline, the Ottoman Empire remained a significant military power in the region.

    - The Moroccan Question
        The Anglo-French Entente focused on resolving Mediterranean disputes, including control over territories such as Morocco, Bosnia, Herzegovina, Tripoli, and the Balkan Peninsula.
        8.1. France’s Blunder and Germany’s Reaction
            •	The Entente granted France a free hand in Morocco, but French Foreign Minister Delcassé failed to consult Germany on the matter.
            •	German Chancellor Bernhard von Bülow took advantage of this to assert that Morocco was an international issue, not just a French concern.
            •	Germany also used the dispute to test the strength of the Anglo-French Entente.
        8.2. Kaiser Wilhelm II’s Intervention (March 1895)
            •	Kaiser Wilhelm II publicly challenged France’s role in Morocco, delivering a speech to the German colony in Tangier:
                •	He emphasized Morocco’s independence and called for an open-door policy for international trade, opposing monopolies or annexation.
            •	In April 1895, Bülow escalated tensions by demanding a conference of the Treaty of Madrid (1880) signatories to discuss Morocco.
        8.3. The Algeciras Conference (1906)
            •	Sultan Abdul Aziz IV of Morocco invited the concerned parties to a conference.
            •	French Foreign Minister Delcassé resisted but eventually conceded under pressure from U.S. President Theodore Roosevelt, marking the first major U.S. intervention in European diplomacy.
            •	The conference was held in Algeciras (January–April 1906).
        8.4. Outcomes of the Conference
            •	France and Spain jointly gained control over the Moroccan police.
            •	Germany ensured that the Moroccan issue remained a multilateral concern.
            •	The conference had broader geopolitical effects:
                •	Strengthened ties between Britain, France, and Russia.
                •	Marked the rise of the U.S. as a world power.
                •	Continued Anglo-French military consultations until 1914, despite changes in the British government.

    - The Anglo-Russian Convention (1907)
        The Anglo-Russian Convention, signed on 31 August 1907, marked the end of longstanding rivalries between Britain and Russia, bringing them to an agreement on key territorial and political issues in Persia, Afghanistan, and Tibet.
        9.1. Persia: Spheres of Influence
            •	Persia was divided into three zones:
                •	A large Russian sphere in the north.
                •	A small British sphere in the southeast.
                •	A neutral zone in between, where both powers were placed on equal footing.
            •	Both countries agreed to maintain the status quo in the Persian Gulf.
        9.2. Afghanistan: British Supremacy Recognized
            •	Britain assured Russia that it would not take any action threatening Russian interests in Afghanistan.
            •	Russia, in turn, agreed to conduct all political relations with Kabul only through the British Foreign Office.
        9.3. Tibet: Non-Interference Policy
            •	Both nations agreed to respect Tibet’s territorial integrity.
            •	They also pledged not to interfere in Tibet’s internal administration.
        The Anglo-Russian Convention marked a significant step in the formation of the Triple Entente, aligning Britain, France, and Russia against the Triple Alliance (Germany, Austria-Hungary, and Italy) in the lead-up to World War I.

    - The Awakening of the Far East
        At the turn of the 19th century, the Far East gained global significance, with the balance of power shifting in favor of Japan. 
            Japan pursued regional supremacy, challenging European interests, particularly in China and Korea.
        10.1. The Boxer Rebellion and Its Aftermath (1900-1901)
            •	The rise of ultra-nationalist Boxers, supported by the Dowager Empress Cixi (1835-1908), threatened foreigners in China.
            •	European powers, setting aside their rivalries, sent an international force to suppress the rebellion in 1900.
            •	The Boxer settlement (September 1901) imposed severe penalties on China:
                •	Emperor Guangxu apologized to Berlin and Tokyo for the murder of German and Japanese diplomats.
                •	China was forced to pay an indemnity of 450,000,000 Taels.
                •	Harsh punishments were imposed:
                    •	Two Imperial princes were sentenced to death.
                    •	Three high-ranking officials were ordered to commit suicide.
                    •	Three leading Mandarins were beheaded.
        10.2. The Anglo-Japanese Alliance (1902)
            •	In January 1902, Britain and Japan signed an agreement to safeguard their special interests in China and Korea.
            •	The treaty allowed Britain to shift its naval focus from the Pacific to the North Sea and Atlantic, where Germany’s growing naval power posed a challenge.
            •	Japan’s international standing soared, reaching its peak in 1905 when it defeated Russia decisively in the Russo-Japanese War.
        This period marked Japan’s emergence as a dominant power in Asia, while European influence in the Far East began to wane.

    - The Russo-Japanese War and the Treaty of Portsmouth (1904-1905)
        By the turn of the 20th century, Russian influence was expanding in northern China. This expansion, particularly in Manchuria and Korea, alarmed Japan and led to rising tensions between the two powers.
        11.1. Rising Tensions Between Russia and Japan
            •	Russian Expansion in Manchuria and Korea
                •	Russia deployed troops into Manchuria and constructed railways linking Vladivostok and Port Arthur under permission from the Chinese government.
                •	In winter 1902-03, Russia increased its activities in North Korea, moving troops towards the Yalu River under the pretext of protecting Russian nationals cutting timber under a Chinese concession.
            •	Failed Diplomatic Negotiations
                •	Negotiations between Russia and Japan led to an agreement in January 1904:
                    •	Japan agreed to stay out of Manchuria.
                    •	Russia agreed to stay out of Korea.
                •	However, Russia failed to respond within the stipulated three weeks, leading Japan to sever diplomatic relations.
        11.2. The Russo-Japanese War (1904-1905)
            •	Contrary to European expectations, Japan was well-prepared:
                •	Its army was expanded, reorganized, and re-equipped.
                •	Its navy was highly efficient and well-organized.
                •	Japan had a geographical advantage, whereas Russia struggled to reinforce its Far Eastern troops.
            •	Economic Backing
                •	Japan received financial support from London and New York.
                •	Russia relied on France.
            •	Major Battles and Japanese Victories
                •	Land Battles: Japan defeated Russian forces in the battles of Liaoyang, Shaho, and Mukden (20 February – 10 March 1905).
                •	Naval Battles:
                    •	Admiral Togo led the destruction of Russian sea power in Port Arthur.
                    •	He inflicted a crushing defeat on Russia’s Baltic Fleet in the Battle of Tsushima (27-28 May 1905).
        11.3. The Treaty of Portsmouth (5 September 1905)
            By summer 1905, Japan had achieved its war objectives, leading to a peace settlement brokered by the United States. 
            The Treaty of Portsmouth (signed 5 September 1905) included:
            •	Russian Recognition of Japanese Dominance
                •	Russia recognized Japan’s paramount interests in Korea.
                •	Russia transferred the Liao-Tung Peninsula lease and the southern Chinese Eastern Railway (Port Arthur to Changchun) to Japan, subject to Chinese approval.
                •	Russia ceded the southern half of Sakhalin Island to Japan.
            •	Withdrawal from Manchuria
                •	Both sides agreed to withdraw their forces from Manchuria and not use railways there for strategic purposes.
        11.4. Consequences of the War
            •	Impact on Russia
                •	The defeat led to domestic rebellion, planting the seeds of the Bolshevik Revolution (1917).
            •	Impact on European Power Balance
                •	Germany’s military strategists saw war on two fronts (as in 1914) as more feasible after Russia’s defeat.
            •	Impact on Nationalist Movements
                •	Japan’s victory over a European power inspired nationalist movements in India, Turkey, Persia, and beyond.
            •	The Anglo-Japanese Alliance (1905)
                •	Britain recognized Japan’s dominance in Korea.
                •	Japan acknowledged Britain’s interests in India’s frontier regions.
        The Russo-Japanese War marked the first time an Asian power decisively defeated a European one, shifting the global balance of power and reinforcing Japan’s role as a dominant force in East Asia.

    The Manchurian Crisis
        •	Japanese Expansion in Korea
            •	Agreement with Korea (17 November 1905)
                •	Japan signed an agreement with Korea.
                •	Prince Ito was appointed as the Resident-General at Seoul.
            •	Assassination of Prince Ito (1909)
                •	Killed by a Korean nationalist.
                •	Rising hostility due to Japanese overbearing and exploitative conduct.
            •	Annexation of Korea (22 August 1910)
                •	Japan formally annexed Korea.
                •	Tacit approval from Russia and Great Britain.
        •	Japanese Expansion in Manchuria
            •	Impact of the Russo-Japanese War
                •	Japan’s victory over Russia strengthened its position in Manchuria.
                •	China was forced to accept the terms of the Portsmouth Treaty.
            •	Sino-Japanese Agreement (22 December 1905)
                •	China agreed to open sixteen towns in Manchuria to foreign trade and residence.
                •	Japan was allowed to reconstruct railways connecting Antung (Korean frontier) with Mukden.
            •	Friction and Delays
                •	Reconstruction was completed only in 1911 due to tensions.
                •	China tried to involve Britain and the US by granting railway concessions to British-American companies.
        •	US Intervention and Its Failure
            •	US Proposal (1909)
                •	Suggested an international loan to China to buy foreign-controlled Manchurian railways.
                •	Aimed at ensuring railway neutralization.
            •	Rejection by Russia and Japan
                •	Russia and Japan jointly rejected the US proposal.
                •	Showed that the US was still far from becoming a world power.
        •	Growing Japan-US Tensions
            •	Speculation of Conflict
                •	Rising tensions over immigration issues between Japan and the US.
            •	Third Anglo-Japanese Alliance (1911)
                •	Strengthened Japan’s position in the Far East.
        •	China’s Political Collapse
        •	Government Breakdown
            •	Administrative failure across all provinces.
        •	Fall of the Manchu Dynasty (1912)
            •	Yuan Shih-Kai took power.
            •	Established a new regime, ending imperial rule in China.

    Crisis in the Near East
        1. The Aftermath of the Treaty of Berlin (1878)
            •	The Treaty of Berlin settled Near Eastern affairs, with little change except for Egypt coming under British rule.
            •	The Ottoman Empire, though in decline, failed to capitalize on the internal divisions within the Balkans.
            •	Britain’s acquisition of Cyprus and dominance over Egypt reduced Ottoman Turkey’s strategic importance.
            •	Russia, after the Treaty of Portsmouth (1905), shifted focus from the Near East to the Middle East, creating a power vacuum that Germany sought to exploit.
        2. The Armenian Massacres and German Influence
            •	During the late 19th century, Kaiser Wilhelm II’s interest in the region became evident, particularly during the Armenian massacres.
            •	The massacres at Urfa (September 1895) and Constantinople (August 1896) were orchestrated under Sultan Abdul Hamid II, with Turkish soldiers and fanatics carrying out the atrocities.
            •	The British public reacted strongly, but Salisbury’s Conservative government refrained from intervention to avoid a European war.
            •	The crisis strengthened German influence in Constantinople while weakening Britain’s position, setting the stage for World War I.
        3. The Macedonian Crisis (1903-1908)
            •	Turkish misrule in Macedonia led to tensions, prompting Austria and Russia to agree on the Murzsteg reforms in 1903, dividing Macedonia into administrative zones.
            •	Austria’s 1908 proposal to build a railway through the Sanjak of Novibazar raised Russian suspicions, ending their cooperation in the Balkans.
            •	In July 1908, the Young Turk Revolution, led by Enver Pasha, overthrew Sultan Abdul Hamid II and established a parliamentary system.
            •	The new Parliament’s inclusion of representatives from Bosnia and Herzegovina alarmed Vienna, as Austria-Hungary formally occupied these provinces.
        4. The Annexation of Bosnia and Herzegovina (1908)
            •	Serbia, under King Peter I, sought the unification of Southern Slavs, but Austria-Hungary swiftly moved to annex Bosnia and Herzegovina.
            •	A secret agreement between Austria and Russia allowed the annexation in exchange for Russian access to the Bosphorus and the Dardanelles.
            •	On 5 October 1908, Bulgaria declared independence, and the next day, Austria-Hungary annexed Bosnia and Herzegovina.
            •	Serbia, diplomatically isolated, had to accept the annexation in March 1909.
            •	German Chancellor Bülow hailed this as proof of the Austro-German alliance’s strength in continental politics.
        5. The Moroccan Crisis (1908-1912)
            •	In 1908, Sultan Abdul Aziz IV of Morocco was overthrown by his brother Mulai Hafid, but his rule was unstable.
            •	The Riff tribes rebelled against Spain in 1909, forcing Spain to deploy 50,000 troops.
            •	In 1911, revolts around Fez led France to intervene, occupying the city while Spain seized Larache.
            •	Germany, angered by these developments, sent the warship Panther to Agadir, challenging the Treaty of Algeciras.
            •	Britain reacted sharply, leading to negotiations where Germany backed down.
            •	In 1912, France established a protectorate over Morocco, ceding parts of the French Congo to Germany in return.
            •	The crisis left lasting tensions between Britain, France, and Germany.
        6. The Italo-Turkish War (1911-1912)
            •	Italy, fearing French expansion in North Africa, declared war on Turkey in September 1911.
            •	Using Turkish interference in trade as a pretext, Italy seized Tripoli.
            •	Britain denied Turkey permission to send reinforcements through Egypt, allowing Italy an easy victory.
            •	The Treaty of Ouchy (October 1912) resulted in Turkey ceding Tripoli to Italy.
            •	Italy’s shift from the Triple Alliance to the Triple Entente was a crucial development for World War I.
        7. Formation of the Balkan League (1912)
            •	Bulgaria’s King Ferdinand and Prime Minister Gueshoff sought cooperation with Serbia to realize Bulgaria’s territorial ambitions.
            •	With Russian support, Serbia and Bulgaria signed a treaty in March 1912, joined by Greece in May and Montenegro in August.
            •	A secret annex in the treaty provided for joint military action against Turkey under Russian approval.
        8. The First Balkan War (1912-1913)
            •	Turkish promises of reform in Albania dissatisfied Serbia and Bulgaria.
            •	Montenegro mobilized, and by 3 November 1912, Balkan Christian states were at war with Turkey.
            •	The Bulgarians advanced toward Constantinople, forcing Turkey to seek European intervention.
            •	A conference in London led to a peace treaty in December 1912, but Enver Pasha rejected it.
            •	Fighting resumed, with Adrianople falling to Bulgarian and Serbian forces while Greece captured Jannina.
            •	The Treaty of May 1913:
                •	Greece gained Salonika, southern Macedonia, and Crete.
                •	Serbia secured northern Macedonia.
                •	Bulgaria received Thrace and the Aegean coast but ceded Silistria to Romania.
                •	Turkey retained Constantinople and eastern Thrace.
        9. The Second Balkan War (1913)
            •	Within a month, Bulgaria attacked Serbian forces in Macedonia and attempted to seize Salonika.
            •	Serbia and Greece resisted, while Romania invaded Bulgaria.
            •	Turkey exploited the situation to retake Adrianople.
            •	The Treaty of Bucharest (August 1913):
                •	Bulgaria lost access to the Aegean Sea and territory in Macedonia.
                •	Romania gained land with 340,000 people.
                •	Turkey retained Adrianople.
                •	Albania was recognized as independent.
        10. Prelude to World War I (1913-1914)
            •	The Balkan Wars proved that Britain and Germany could cooperate to prevent war.
            •	However, military buildup intensified across Europe, with growing tensions in Germany and France.
            •	French Ambassador to Vienna noted the rising belief that war was inevitable.
        11. The Assassination of Archduke Franz Ferdinand (28 June 1914)
            •	The heir to the Habsburg throne was assassinated in Sarajevo, triggering a chain of events leading to World War I.
            •	Austria issued an ultimatum to Serbia on 23 July 1914, demanding compliance within 48 hours.
            •	On 31 July, Germany gave Russia an ultimatum to halt mobilization within 12 hours.
            •	By 1 August, Germany and Russia were at war, followed by Germany’s declaration of war on France on 3 August.
            •	Germany’s invasion of Belgium provoked Britain, which declared war on 4-5 August.
            •	Bismarck’s 1891 prediction that a world war would start in the East proved correct.

    - World War I
        Causes of World War I
            The immediate provocation for World War I was the assassination of Archduke Francis Ferdinand. 
            However, several underlying causes had been brewing for over a generation, leading to the outbreak of the war:
                Extreme Nationalism
                    Inflamed racial pride among people of different nations.
                    Increased rivalries between states.
                    Led to disputes over colonial possessions in Asia, Africa, and the Balkans.
                    Nationalist aspirations in the Balkans turned the region into a volatile "tinderbox" that ultimately ignited the war.
                Formation of Alliances
                    Nations grouped into mutually suspicious alliances, increasing tensions.
                    Militarism, along with secret treaties, intensified rivalries, setting the stage for war.
                Imperialism and Economic Conflicts
                    Competition for markets, raw materials, and investment opportunities.
                    Germany’s unfulfilled imperialist ambitions played a major role in the war’s outbreak in 1914.
        Character and Progress of World War I
            When war broke out, it became unprecedented in scale and destruction:
                Nearly all known nation-states participated, and battles were fought worldwide.
                Lethal weaponry, including submarines, Zeppelins, poison gas, tanks, and armored vehicles, led to massive casualties.
                Like many European wars before, it was fought for the "Balance of Power."
                Two opposing sides emerged:
                    Central Powers: Germany, Austria-Hungary, Bulgaria, and Turkey.
                    Allied Powers: France, England, Japan, and Italy.
        Key Developments:
            For the first three and a half years, Germany held the upper hand.
            In 1917, Russia withdrew from the war after the Bolsheviks overthrew the Tsarist regime, further strengthening Germany.
            Germany's large-scale submarine warfare (U-boat menace) threatened Allied control of the seas.
            The United States entered the war after a German submarine sank the American ship Lusitania, turning the tide in favor of the Allies.
            Germany surrendered in November 1918 due to combined Allied pressure.
            The Allies quickly defeated Turkey, secured Bulgaria's surrender, and witnessed Austria's collapse.
        End of the War:
            President Woodrow Wilson of the USA proposed the "Fourteen Points" as the basis for a peace settlement.
            A naval mutiny in Germany led to the Kaiser fleeing to Holland.
            A republic was established in Germany, which signed the armistice on 11 November 1918, ending the war.
        The Paris Peace Conference of 1919
            Held in Paris in 1919, attended by the victorious Allied powers.
            The venue selection reflected a spirit of vengeance.
            The conference had to consider various secret treaties made during the war regarding territorial distributions.
            French Prime Minister Clemenceau and British Premier Lloyd George pressured Wilson into compromising on his Fourteen Points.
            Resulted in a series of five treaties concluded in and around Paris.
        The Treaty of Versailles, 1919
            Signed between the Allies and Germany on 28 June 1919, the Treaty of Versailles imposed harsh conditions on Germany:
                Territorial Losses:
                    Alsace-Lorraine was returned to France.
                    Saar Valley’s coal fields were given to France for 15 years.
                    Eupen and Malmedy were ceded to Belgium after a plebiscite.
                    Schleswig was returned to Denmark following a plebiscite.
                    West Prussia was used to create a Polish corridor to the Baltic.
                    Memel was given to Lithuania, and Prussian Poland was annexed to Poland.
                    Danzig was internationalized under League of Nations control.
                Colonial and Overseas Losses:
                    Germany lost all African colonies and privileges in China, which were placed under the League of Nations.
                Military Restrictions:
                    German army reduced to 100,000 men; conscription abolished.
                    Banned the production and trade of war materials.
                    German navy was limited and prohibited from having submarines.
                    The Rhineland was demilitarized.
                War Guilt and Reparations:
                    Germany accepted full responsibility for the war.
                    Had to pay huge financial reparations.
                    Allied forces occupied the left bank of the Rhine until treaty terms were met.
                The Treaty of Versailles was a "dictated peace" as German representatives had no say in its terms.
        Other Treaties
            Treaty of St. Germain (1919): Signed with Austria-Hungary, dismembering the empire:
                Bohemia and Moravia formed Czechoslovakia.
                Bosnia and Herzegovina merged with Serbia to form Yugoslavia.
                Italy received South Tyrol, Trentino, and parts of the Adriatic coast.
                Poland received Galicia, and Hungary became independent.
            Treaty of Neuilly (1919): Bulgaria lost:
                Western Thrace to Greece.
                Part of Macedonia to Yugoslavia.
                Dobruja to Romania.
            Treaty of Trianon (1920): Hungary ceded:
                Transylvania to Romania.
                Slovakia to Czechoslovakia.
                Croatia to Yugoslavia, losing all sea access.
            Treaty of Sèvres (1920): Signed with Turkey:
                Lost all rights in Africa and the Middle East.
                Greece occupied Eastern Thrace, Smyrna, and parts of Asia Minor.
                Armenia was to become independent.
                Dardanelles and Bosphorus became neutral zones.
                Turkish nationalist leader Mustafa Kemal rejected the treaty, leading to its replacement by the Treaty of Lausanne.
        Results of the War
            Collapse of four major empires: German, Austro-Hungarian, Ottoman, and Russian.
            Rise of new republics and smaller nation-states.
            Spread of democratic ideals, but also rise of totalitarian ideologies (Bolshevism, Fascism, and Nazism).
            Formation of the League of Nations to resolve future conflicts.
            Advances in labor rights, women's suffrage, medicine, aviation, and military technology.
        Impact of World War I on India
            India actively supported Britain:
                300,000 Indian soldiers fought in West Asia, Africa, and Europe.
                800,000 more were later recruited.
                Supplied vast amounts of food, materials, and funds.
                Contributed £100 million to Britain’s war costs.
            Britain’s Response:
                Promised self-governance (Montagu Declaration, 20 August 1917).
                Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms (Government of India Act, 1919) introduced dyarchy.
                Failed to satisfy Indian aspirations, especially after oppressive measures:
                    Rowlatt Act: Allowed arrests without trial.
                    Jallianwala Bagh Massacre (1919): British troops killed peaceful protestors.
                    Khilafat Movement: Discontent over British treatment of Turkey.
            Rise of Indian nationalism led by Mahatma Gandhi, ultimately culminating in India's independence on 15 August 1947.

    The Russian Revolution, 1917
        1. Introduction
            During World War I, two significant events occurred in 1917:
                •	The entry of the United States into the war, which ultimately influenced its outcome.
                •	The Russian Revolution, which marked the end of the Tsarist regime and introduced the “dictatorship of the proletariat”.
            The Russian Revolution was the most significant social upheaval since the French Revolution.
        2. Circumstances Leading to the Russian Revolution
            The revolution, epitomized by the Bolshevik seizure of power in Petrograd on 7 November 1917, had long been in the making due to:
                •	The Russo-Japanese War (1905), which weakened confidence in the Tsarist regime.
                •	Russian military defeats in World War I, leading to further distrust.
                •	Widespread discontent with the inefficient and autocratic rule of the Tsar.
        3. The First Revolution (March 1917)
            Initially, Russia entered the war with enthusiasm and saw some early successes. However, by 1915:
                •	Military defeats exposed the regime’s weaknesses.
                •	Munitions and supplies were critically low.
                •	Tsar Nicholas II was weak and influenced by corrupt ministers.
                •	Rumors of a separate peace with Germany fueled unrest.
                •	Rasputin’s influence over the royal family worsened the crisis, leading to his assassination.
            Key Events:
                •	Riots erupted among peasants and workers.
                •	Desertions increased within the army.
                •	Food shortages intensified, particularly a shortage of bread.
                •	The Petrograd Soviet (workers’ and soldiers’ council) was formed.
                •	The Duma (parliament) established a Provisional Government, forcing Tsar Nicholas II to abdicate.
        4. The Provisional Government
            The Provisional Government, led by moderate republicans (Kadets), implemented:
                •	Liberal reforms (freedom of press, speech, and religion).
                •	Plans for a National Constituent Assembly.
                •	Commitment to continuing the war, hoping to maintain patriotic support.
            However, the masses prioritized:
                •	Peace
                •	Land reforms
                •	Food security
            Growing Discontent:
                •	The revolution shifted from a political movement to a socialist one.
                •	Local Soviets emerged across Russia, mobilizing workers, peasants, and soldiers.
                •	Workers demanded better wages and working conditions.
                •	Peasants seized noble lands.
                •	Soldiers mutinied, and national minorities (Finns, Poles) sought independence.
        5. The Second Revolution (November 1917)
            The Rise of the Bolsheviks:
                •	The failure of the Provisional Government to end the war led to dissatisfaction.
                •	Mensheviks, under Alexander Kerensky, came to power but failed to bring peace.
                •	The Bolsheviks, led by Lenin and Trotsky, opposed the war and advocated immediate socialist reforms.
            The Bolshevik Coup:
                •	Germans advanced towards Petrograd, creating instability.
                •	Bolshevik influence grew within the Petrograd Soviet.
                •	In November 1917, the Bolsheviks overthrew the Provisional Government.
                •	Kerensky fled, and Bolsheviks seized power.
        6. Consolidation of Bolshevik Rule\
            Immediate Actions:
                •	Lenin negotiated the Treaty of Brest-Litovsk (1918), ending Russia’s involvement in World War I.
                •	Territorial losses were accepted as a necessary price for revolution.
            Key Reforms:
                •	Abolition of private property.
                •	Peasants received land to be cultivated for the state.
                •	Factories were handed over to workers.
                •	Compulsory labor was introduced.
                •	Public debts were repudiated.
                •	The Orthodox Church was stripped of power.
            Repression:
                •	The Cheka (secret police) executed opposition figures, including Tsar Nicholas II and his family (July 1918).
                •	The Red Army, led by Trotsky, crushed anti-Bolshevik forces, ensuring Bolshevik rule for over 70 years.
        7. Causes of the Russian Revolution
            The revolution resulted from long-term dissatisfaction across Russian society:
            1. Peasants
                •	Suffered economic insecurity and social oppression.
                •	Small land holdings vs. large aristocratic estates led to resentment.
                •	Formed the bulk of the Social Revolutionary Party.
            2. Workers
                •	Low wages and harsh working conditions led to discontent.
                •	Many joined the Workmen’s Social Democratic Party.
            3. Middle Class (Bourgeoisie)
                •	Wanted constitutional government to limit autocratic rule.
            4. Intellectuals
                •	Advocated radical political and social reform.
            5. Minority Nationalities (Jews, Poles, Finns, etc.)
                •	Resisted Tsarist policies of Russification and sought independence.
            6. Influence of Ideologies
                •	Moderate republicanism and Marxian socialism fueled revolutionary movements.
            These diverse elements ultimately overthrew the Tsarist regime, leading to the establishment of a socialist state under the Bolsheviks.


- The world from 1919 to 1939

    World War I, or the Great War as it was then described, was called the ‘war to end all wars’. It was, till then, the most devastating conflict in human history. More than 37 million combatants from all the countries were killed with nothing substantial achieved by any of the original parties to the War. Still, it ended on a note of hope. There was wide expectation that humanity would learn a lesson from the horrors of this War. Conflicts over territories, it was hoped, would henceforth be resolved through a structured dispute-settlement process. There was optimism that the world would be ‘safe for democracy’ and there would be equity and progress for all.

    However, the opposite happened. Europe, and because the continent along with the United States was the political and economic fulcrum of the world, quickly took another plunge into further chaos and disillusionment.

    Colonialism remained intact, keeping more than half of the world’s population shackled. All the retrogressive institutions of the pre-War world — aristocracy, feudalism, supra nationalism and militarism— remained intact while Fascism and Communism - rose as new factors pushing Europe, and through it the whole world, to another calamitous war within the space of a generation. Therefore, the world in the period between 1919 and 1939, is rightly called the ‘inter-War’ period. In this Chapter we will analyse the careers of the world powers and their close allies during this important period in history and understand their individual contributions to the global slide towards World War II.

    The League or Nations
        A discussion on the world between the two World Wars cannot begin without a close look on mankind's first attempt at constructing a global bulwark against armed conflict —- the League of Nations. This was the world’s first ever forum of nations, carrying the burden of a collective desire that there should be an acceptable arbiter in conflicts between nations without compromising national sovereignty.

        The League of Nations was, originally, the dream of the US President Woodrow Wilson (1913-21), who included it in his famous Fourteen Points that he proposed on 18 January 1918. The victorious powers incorporated the Covenant of the League of Nations in the final treaties, which brought the curtains down on World War I. It was to be a forum in which all the states of the world, small or big, would cooperate for the preservation of peace and reduce their war-waging capabilities.

        The Covenant of the League of Nations was a list of rules by which the League was to operate. It was drawn up by an international committee, which comprised leading statesmen like Lord Robert Cecil of Britain, Jan Smuts of South Africa and Leon Bourgeois of France. President Woodrow Wilson of the US insisted that the Covenant should be included in each of the separate peace treaties signed at the end of the World War I. This ensured that the League actually came into existence rather than remain a mere topic of discussion.

        However, the League was stricken with contradictions from the very outset. There was no clear statement in its

        Charter against Colonialism. In fact, it lengthened the humiliation of colonised countries like India by providing for the creation of ‘tutelages’ or a system of mandate by which the colonies would be placed in the charge of one of the great powers and gradually be guided up the road to their independence. Not only was this provision vague, it also lacked a neutral enforcement machinery. The League also made quite a few compromises on this score. To persuade Japan, an emerging power- to join it, allowed Tokyo to take over the former German concessions in China. To placate the French, the Germans and the Russians were kept out, thereby reducing the impact of the League considerably. The United States refused to be part of it. So three of the world’s most important powers had no association with the League.

        Headquartered in Geneva, Switzerland, the League of Nations had its own bureaucracy and quickly evolved a set of traditions in international legislation making. Its main organs were a General Assembly, the Council with four permanent members (Britain, France, Italy and Japan), a Permanent Court of International Justice based at the Hague in Netherlands, a Secretariat, numerous committees to work out mandates, resolve disputes and address vital issues like disarmament. The League also had a Peace-keeping arm. The still existing International Labour Organisation was set up under a League mandate. It also had a Refugee Organisation and a Health Organisation, which may be regarded as precursors of the present United Nations’ organisations with similar functions.

        Therefore, the League had a lasting impact because it created a model for the United Nations, which came into existence after World War II, to emulate. But during its short career, it failed to make an impact on any of the disputes which countries got caught in. When Poland backed by France seized the Vilna area from Lithuania in 1920, the League remained unresponsive. In 1923, Italy and Greece were on the brink of war. This arose from the infamous Corfu incident in which three Italian ‘officials working on a boundary commission were murdered. The Italian dictator, Benitto Mussolini, blamed the Greeks and demanded payment of a huge compensation. When the Greeks failed to pay, the Italian Air Force carried out bombing missions on the Greek island of Corfu. When Greece appealed to the League, it did not play a role. Rather, Britain and France played the joint role of mediator. In 1931, Japan made a mockery of the League by seizing Manchuria from China. When Italy conquered Ethiopia in 1936, the League did nothing. In fact, by the time Adolf Hitler had emerged as a pan-European threat, the old system of secret pacts and power balancing treaties returned. The League had been rendered totally irrelevant. We now turn our attention to how individual countries coped during the difficult inter-war years and played their own small parts in making it one of the most significant periods of world history.

    GERMANY
        The provisions of Versailles left a permanent scar on the German pride. It is sometimes suggested that the seeds of World War II were thus planted in Versailles - humiliated and battered people could only be expected to extract revenge in the long run. What fuelled this logic was the feeling widespread among the Germans that it had been ‘stabbed in the back’ by its Generals and communists in the closing stages of World War I. However, the truth is quite different. Vengeful action, at least up to that point in history, was not a rare thing in post-conflict European situations. The Congress of Vienna (1815) had made France pay heavily for the Napoleanic wars. Germany had itself treated France with undue harshness in 1871. And, it should not be forgotten that when Russia sued for peace in March 1918, the victorious German Supreme Command had imposed on it the Treaty of Brest Litovsk under which Russia was forced to give up a territory nearly as large as Austria-Hungary and Turkey combined, with 56 million people, a third of its railway network, 73 per cent of its iron ore reserves, 89 per cent of its coal, more than 5,000 factories and an indemnity of more than 6 billion marks. In comparison, Versailles had let off Germany lightly. At least the country was left geographically and economically largely intact. Germany could preserve its political unity and its potential as a great nation.

        Whatever the fine truth, the German saga for the inter-war years was one of revival of the old Prussian militarism combined with a drift towards totalitarianism. After 1918, Germany resembled a house divided. The huge burden of reparation caused resentment among the people, a - sentiment on which the conservative forces fed. Germany’s Chancellor, Philip Scheidemann described the Treaty as an ‘imprisonment of the German people’, but had no choice but to accept it. The Social Democrats and the Catholic Centre Party formed a new government and their representatives signed the Treaty. These parties made up the Weimar Republic, so named because of the town in which the constitution of the new Germany was written in 1919. The Republic lasted till the advent of Adolf Hitler to power in 1933 and carried the stigma of accepting the Treaty of Versailles. But they had no choice because the victorious Allies were threatening Germany with a complete military takeover unless the Treaty was accepted in whole.

        The Weimar Constitution was fairly a liberal document and promised a new career to Germany as the government comprised of men of unique talents. Civil liberties were guaranteed to all Germans. The government was to be formed by universal adult suffrage. However, there were also some contradictions. The Reichstag or Parliament, had a system of proportional representation. This made it simple for small parties to gain seats and destabilise the government with their tendency to alter combinations at their will. Moreover, though the ministers were responsible to the Reichstag, the President appointed and removed the Chancellor, who was head of the cabinet. There was ample scope for imposition of presidential dictatorship as the President was empowered to rule by decree during national emergencies.

        The Weimar Republic faced problems from the very beginning. The communists, backed by the Bolsheviks of Moscow, dreamt of using the country’s instability to stage a revolution mirroring the one in Russia called the Spartacist uprising. Led by Rosa Luxemburg and Kar] Liebknecht, they tried to organise a proletariat revolution in 1919. But the Army crushed this attempt. Luxemburg and Liebknecht were shot dead soon after their capture, In the following year, the right-wing Kapp Putsch (armed insurrection) was attempted in Berlin. This was the conservatives’ attempt to take the country back to the days of Prussian aristocratic domination, This attempt failed but not until all important government officers fled Berlin leaving a vacuum to be filled by the workers. This was followed by strikes in all the cities, to suppress which the Army had to be called in. Such extremism, coming from the Left as well as the Right, haunted the Weimar Republic throughout its career. Adding to Germany's misery was a series of assassinations of important Republican leaders, some of whom were quite progressive.

        What inspired totalitarianism most was the country’s woeful economic condition. Inflation took over the country when the government printed huge amounts of currency to finance its programmes. In 1921, the Allies presented Germany with a bill for war damages totalling over $ 33 billion, which the Weimar Republic was forced to accept under threat of invasion, The French invasion of the Ruhr basin in January 1923 to secure the payment of war reparations was met with passive resistance by the Weimar government which asked the Ruhr labotr force to go on strike in return for payment of subsidies. Though the crisis was overcome within a few months, it inspired radical forces in the country to oppose the French occupation. To help them, there was widespread unemployment, (approximately 6 million in the mid-1920s). Those with jobs fared no better because their salaries could not keep pace with rising prices. Those who had to live off fixed incomes suffered the most. When the great Wall Street crash occurred in the USA in 1929, the German economy suffered the most as the revival of its economy was indexed largely on cheap exports as per. the recommendations of the Dawes Committee. The Americans had made huge investments in Germany during the late 1920s inspired by the Dawes Plan (1924) and the Young Plan (1929). Agricultural prices collapsed leaving the farmers angry. University students were disillusioned by the growing unemployment situation.

        The Weimar Republic was led by men of exceptional talents. Under Gustav Stresemann’s brief Chancellorship in 1923, the country acquired political stability. He recruited an able economist called Hjalmar Schacht at a time when the value of the Mark was at its lowest. Schacht introduced a new currency called the Rentenmark which led to considerable strengthening of the German monetary system. In 1924, Germany and the Allies negotiated a new method of reparation payments. The French Army withdrew from the Ruhr region. Stresemann, who resigned as Chancellor to become the foreign minister, won back international respectability for Germany by adopting a policy of reconciliation with its former enemies-— Britain and France. Germany also joined the League of Nations. Stresemann's biggest achievement was getting the British and French accept the Treaty of Locarno (1926) under which they agreed to formalise the German border in the west, while the status of the east was left unclear. The latter arrangement suited German interests because by this time there was considerable public opinion in favour of regaining the territories lost at Versailles to Poland and Czechoslovakia. But most political parties in Germany, one of them being Adolf Hitler's Nazi party, rejected Locarno as an extension of Versailles. Though by the end of the 1920s, the Weimar government was steadily winning the hearts of the Germans with a new liberalism and giving the country a firmer economic footing with its reforms programme, the Great Depression in America and its disastrous effects on the European, particularly German economy, gave retrogressive forces their big chance to return to the political centre-stage with totalitarian agendas

        The Rise of Nazism
            Adolf Hitler made his first appearance in the German political scene in 1923. This personality’s rise to power was an unprecedented success story. In the words of William L. Shirer, the famous American journalist-historian, “considering his (Hitler's) origins and early life, it would be difficult to imagine a more unlikely figure to succeed the mantle of Bismarck, the Hohenzollern emperors and President Hindenburg than this singular Austrian of peasant stock.” He was born the son of an ordinary Austrian customs official in 1889, had an incomplete formal education and spent most of his youth as a vagabond on the streets of Vienna. In World War I, he fought in the German Army as an ordinary soldier with exceptional valour for which he received the highest military honours. When the War ended he was back to a purposeless life, but fired with the sense of humiliation and feelings for revenge, which most Germans suffered. So, he drifted into politics.

            In 1919, as an intelligence agent for the German Army, he attended a meeting of a small nationalistic and anti-Semitic group. He joined it more out of curiosity than conviction. In 1920, it adopted the name National Socialist German Workers Party, or Nazi. In its original political statement, called the Twenty-five Points, the party demanded the repudiation of the Treaty of Versailles, unification of Austria and Germany, exclusion of the Jews from German citizenship, agrarian reforms, which called for prohibition of land speculation, and advocated a broad programme of nationalisation of industries.

            From its very inception, the Nazi party conducted its activities with military fanfare. Its supporters were regimented into paramilitary groups called Sturm Abteilung (SA), which went about in brown shirts. In this way they captivated the ordinary Germans who were nostalgic for the pre-World War I militarist society. The French occupation of the Ruhr valley in 1923 gave the Nazis the opportunity to drum up jingoistic passions. They mocked the policy of passive resistance adopted by the Weimar Republic.

            Hitler was in total control of the Nazi party by this time. On 9 November 1923, he led a band of followers to stage a coup when leaders of the Bavarian legislature in Munich were having a meeting at a Beer Hall. The local authorities crushed the uprising and Hitler was sentenced to five years’ imprisonment. While in jail he wrote his autobiography, Mein Kampf (My Struggle). He spent less than a year in jail before being paroled. The Beer Hall putsch taught Hitler a valuable lesson. Henceforth, he started adopting legal means to capture power.

            But by the time Hitler was released from jail, parties preaching extreme nationaljsm had become irrelevant, as under the Weimar government a new spirit of reform was sweeping the nation. For the Nazis it meant a long period in the wilderness. They however got their chance when Germany was plunged into a second economic crisis when American investment left the country in the aftermath of the Wall Street crash of 1929, Once again unemployment and economic stagnation crippled the country's economy. In the background there was also political instability. A coalition of centrist parties and the Social Democrats were in power since 1928. When the Great Depression struck, the parties quibbled over the right economic policy to adopt. The Social Democrats demanded that there should be no cuts in social welfare while the centrist parties wanted a balanced budget. The coalition dissolved over this in March 1930. No single party was strong enoiigh to form a government on its own. Pr \ident von Hindenburg, a former Field Marsh: ii who was'a World War I supreme command member, appointed Heinrich Bruning as Chancellor and ruled through presidential decrees.

            But the German economic crisis persisted. In 1932, von Hindenburg dismissed Bruning and appointed Franz von Papen in his place. From the outset, von Papen was interested in drawing in the support of the Nazis because they were by now a major factor in German politics, having secured 30.1 per cent of the votes in the 1930 elections. The party, whic!, had only 12 seats in the Reichstag in 1928, now held 107 seats. Their opposition in the Reichstag was making the democratic functioning of the government impossible.

            So, under Papen’s advice, the President lifted the ban on the Nazis from holding rallies and public meetings. Elections were called in July 1932. The Nazis won 230 seats, polling 37.2 per cent of the votes. Hitler demanded appointment as Chancellor, but Hindenburg refused. Another election was announced in November. But the deadlock continued. The following month, von Papen resigned. Kurt von Schleicher was appointed. This man’s policies were quite unacceptable to Hindenburg because von Schleicher .tied to build up a coalition of conservative groups and trade unionists. This plan, if put to use. was perceived as dangerous. So Hindenburg dismissed von Schleicher and appointed Adlof Hitler as Chancellor on 30 January 1933.

            Hitler followed a three-fold plan to consolidate the Nazis in power. It comprised capturing the legal authority to rule, crushing the country’s political opposition and eliminating rivals within his party. This he implemented with brutal precision. Within a month of coming to power, he used the Reichstag-fire incident to clamp down on the communists. Later, after World War II, evidence was unearthed to show that the Reichstag was burnt down by Nazi agents, who put the blame on the communists and warmed the Germans of a Soviet Union style revolution planned by the communists. So, arming himself with emergency powers, he werit about jailing and murdering key communist leaders and suppressing civil liberties. In March that year, the German Reichstag empowered Hitler. with an Enabling Act, which permitted him to rule through decrees.

            So, the legal instruments were with Hitler now. Next, he turned on rival political parties. He outlawed all existing and potential opposition institutions. By July 1933, the Nazis were left as the only legitimate party in Germany. The governments in the provinces of Germany were also revamped to suit Nazi interests. Opposition politicians were imprisoned, sent to concentration camps.or murdered, Nazi party volunteers made life miserable for ordinary Germans. A system of spying on the common man was introduced as Germany became a police state. People were forced to swear their personal loyalty to Hitler, who was addressed as Der Feuhrer (The Leader). The Nazi salute became a compulsory means of greeting each other. The free press was abolished. Anti-Semitism or hatred for the Jews, was openly practiced. In May 1933, a major show of humiliation was organised against the Jews. Massive bonfires were lit on the streets of Berlin into which books written by Jewish intellectuals were thrown. In 1935, under the Nuremberg Law, German Jews were deprived of their citizenship. Jews were forced out of jobs and their businesses were confiscated. In November 1938, Hitler ordered the attack on Jewish homes, shops and synagogues. This operation is infamous in history as Kristallnacht (night of broken glass).

            The third rung on Hitler's ladder to climb to power was the elimination of potential rivals within the Nazi party. The SA, by the end of Hitler’s first year in power, had grown one million strong. Its leader was Ernst Roehm, an old associate of Hitler. The German officer corps were concerned that the SA had assumed the position of a parallel army threatening their position. Hitler realised that he needed the regular army for his future plans. So he accused Roehm of disloyalty. On 30 June 1934, he ordered the murder of Roehm and key SA leaders. Over the next three days, hundreds of people were killed, including the former Chancellor, von Schleicher. A month after this President von Hindenburg died and Hitler assumed the offices of both the President and the Chancellor.

            As supreme dictator, Hitler was now free to pursue active militarisation of Germany. He began to rebuild the Army, Navy and Luftwaffe (Air Force) virtually from scratch. This was in contravention to the terms of the Versailles treaty, but none of the Allied powers resisted this as by this time they were too involved in their domestic affairs. In 1936, he ordered his Army into the Rhineland. Though France had the strongest Army in Europe at the time and had the legal right to throw the Germans out because Hitler had violated the Versailles and Locarno treaties, it did not take any steps. This emboldened Hitler further. He articulated a new spirit of German expansionism in the east, calling it the right to Lebensraum, or right to living space for Germans.

            Thus, Germany turned full circle within two decades of the end of World War I. It once again emerged as a threat to peace in Europe. The Allied powers were too preoccupied with their domestic problems to intervene. At home, Hitler infused the German people with the ideas of racism which convinced them that they were ‘Aryans’ or a breed of superior people with the right to rule over ‘inferior’ people in eastern Europe and the Jews. This put Germany firmly on the road to another war. But before we dwell on that, let us consider the events in another country which saw the rise of Fascism- Italy.

    ITALY
        Fascism was a political ideology antithetical to democracy, parlia- mentarism, individual liberty, freedom of expression and state control over businesses. Regarding the state control over business, it collided head-long with communism, the other totalitarian ideology. The word Fascism was derived from the Italian word fasces, which meant the authority of the state over all. The Fascists believed that democratic politics led to the weakening of nations because narrow partisan interests often prevailed over the national cause. Benitto Mussolini, the world’s first fascist dictator, who came to power in Italy in 1922, declared in 1931: “The Fascist conception of the state is all embracing and outside of the state no human or spiritual values can exist.” So Fascist governments, which took over in Italy, Germany and Spain, were necessarily of single-party, characterised by terror, regimentation of society and police surveillance of virtually every citizen.

        Mussolini the son of a blacksmith was born in 1883. He became active in politics in 1912 as a socialist. In 1914, he left the socialists and served in the Army (Italy was on the side of the Allies in the War) and was wounded. After the War, he organised the Fasci di Combattimento (Band of Combatants) in Milan in 1919. Providing the basis for the rise of supra-nationalism in Italy was the general feeling that though Italy had been on the victorious side in World War I, the big Allied powers had denied the territories it had prized. The Parliamentary system of the country was then quite non-functional. The country was caught up in internal turmoil. There were strikes and general unrest. Many Italians feared that the situation was becoming ideal for a communist takeover.

        Mussolini organised his followers into the ‘Black Shirts’ and ordered them to terrorise the Socialists, who were the single most powerful party in the country. The ‘Black Shirts’ attacked strikers and farm workers. This won them the support of the conservatives and the industrialists. In 1921, Mussolini and 34 of his followers got elected to the Italian Chamber of Deputies. This helped them increase the membership of their party. In October 1922, he organised a ‘March to Rome’ in which hundreds of thousands of ‘Black Shirts’ took part. King Victor Emmanuel IJ] was alarmed at this and on 29 October sent Mussolini a telegram asking him to take over as Prime Minister.

        Though he was made Prime Minister, Mussolini's Fascists were in a minority in the Chamber of Deputies. He made it clear to the King that he could not rule effectively unless he was given dictatorial powers. As he enjoyed the backing of the country’s big business houses, the King was forced by circumstances to agree on . 23 November 1922, to grant him such authority, but only for one year during which time he had to bring good governance, Mussolini used this to remove all elected representatives from the lower levels of government and put Fascists in their place. In 1924, he changed the country’s election laws under which the party that won the largest popular vote automatically got two-thirds of the seats in Parliament. Coalition governments were thus put out of fashion. This was useful in the 1925 elections in which the Fascists came to gain complete control over the Chamber of Deputies. This majority was used to end the institution of Parliamentary politics in the country. A series of laws were passed in 1925 and 1926 which gave Mussolini the power to rule through decree and dissolve all other political parties.

        Though he was ruthless against opponents, Mussolini won popularity among the ordinary masses of Italians by starting huge public projects. He gave the workers social security and subsidised their vacations. In 1929, he won over the Catholic Church by giving the Pope sovereignty over the Vatican and made Roman Catholicism the state religion of the country. Though Mussolini was able to put Italy on a strong economic footing by the end of the 1920s, the Great Depression proved too much to tackle. By the mid-1930s, the Dictator was forced to embark on military adventures abroad to secure his position. Thus, the Italians contributed significantly to the unstable security environment of Europe, which took the world towards another great conflict by the end of the decade.

    The Spanish civil war
        Spain had a constitutional monarchy under King Alfonso XIII, who ruled since 1885. In 1923, General Primo de Rivera staged a coup and ruled, with the King's approval, for seven years. Though de Rivera was a dictator, he . was not a fascist. In fact he was quite - progressive and many public works and some of industrialisation was carried out under him. He also resolved the country's problems in Morocco in 1925, Matters were quite smooth in Spain until the Great Depression hit its economy. The country was ruined by a financial crisis and unemployment rose. The Army withdrew its support to de Rivera and he resigned. The King also abdicated and a republic was proclaimed.

        But the new Spanish republic faced some serious problems. Catalonia and the Basque provinces asserted their right to separate. The Roman Catholic Church .was bitterly opposed to the Republic and wanted the King to return. The economic crisis ruined Spain's wine and olive export markets. Iron and steel production fell. Unemployment rose and there was general antipathy towards the new rulers of the country. Fearing a social revolution, the country’s Right Wing (conservative) parties who controlled the Cortes (Parliament), backed by the socialists and middle class radicals made concessions on Catalonia allowing it some autonomy. [t made attempts to raise the wages of the workers, nationalised large estates, and formally separated the Church from the State. But these only complicated things because fissures appeared within the Right Wing over these steps. The pro- Church Ceda party defended the Church and the rich landlords. In 1932, some Army officers tried to overthrow Prime Minister Manuel Azana but this move failed. The Left Wing (shades of communist) parties began an agitational programme aimed at weakening the republic.

        In the 1933 elections, the Azana government was defeated , but a right- wing government dominated by the Ceda was formed. But it was ineffective in bringing back peace to the country. A Fascist party, called the ‘Falange’ was formed under Jose Antonio de Rivera, son of the former dictator. It got help from the Army which started trouble for the government in the colony of Morocco in 1936.

        Thus, began the Spanish Civil War, which lasted till 1939. Terrible atrocities were committed by both sides and thousands of innocent people were killed. It turned out to be a practice war for the world powers. General Franco, who took over the leadership of the Fascists, got help from Hitler and Mussolini who sent arms, men, food and ammunition to help him. The Republicans received direct help from Russia and unofficial support from Britain and France in the form of sending ‘volunteers’ to fight. Finally, Franco captured Madrid in March 1939 and installed himself as Dictator. He kept Spain out of World War II and ruled till 1975. Thus, though a ‘Civil War’, it is described as a ‘Little World War’ because of the.involvement of all big powers of Europe in it. whether directly or indirectly.

    BriTAIn AND FRANCE
        Though Britain emerged victorious from World War I, it was no longer the world’s foremost industrial power. That position it conceded to the United States. Like the rest of Europe, it was affected by economic stagnation. To tackle the situation, the government tried a series of measures like reducing wages in the hope that British goods would become more competitive in the world market because of their affordability. But the policy backfired because the workers organised strikes and industrial unrest. Their resentment ended the political supremacy of the Conservatives and helped the Labour Party to come to power in 1924 and 1929. The Great Depression had a catastrophic impact on the British economy. A national government came to power in 1931 comprising representatives of the Conservative. Libera] and Labour parties.

        Britain faced numerous social and economic problems in the inter-war years. Its women were long denied the right to vote. This was corrected in 1918 by the passing of the Representation of the People’s Act which, though giving suffrage to women. put the age limit at 30 whereas for men it was 21.

        In 1920, there was a slump in British overseas trade which threw thousands into unemployment. The Labour Party started a campaign demanding recasting the industrial system along socialist lines. Massive strikes in the railways and coal rocked the country.

        The Irish problem tore British society down the middle. The British government's policies were resented by both the Conservatives and the Liberals. In the 1918 elections, the old Irish Nationalist Party was defeated and its place was taken by the Sinn Fein. The Sinn Fein’s leaders, Micheal Collins and Arthur Griffith, were committed to Republicanism and set up a Republican government in Ireland. The writ of Britain was ignored. The new government encouraged people to rebel against London's dictates. The British government tried to counter this by otfering to establish two separate governments, each with its parliament at Dublin and Belfast. There was widespread condemnation of this but the British government brutally suppressed public opinion. The condition of the Irish people was miserable. Every night thousands of people hid in bushes for fear of being attacked in their sleep by British forces. This also roused domestic opinion against the government in Britain. The government of David Lloyd George, under pressure from the US, decided to negotiate a settlement. In December 1921, a treaty was signed under which an Irish Free State was founded with British dominion status. Though Griffith and Collins accepted it, the more radical leader, Eamonn de Valera, rejected it. A civil war broke out in 1922 between the Republicans and those who had accepted the 1921 treaty with Britain.

        The inter-war period saw the economic muscle of Britain. built up through the 19th century. get considerably reduced. Though the country was politically stable despite strikes and industrial closures, Britain lost its leadership in the industrialised world. Before World War I, almost all countries had been customers of British textiles. heavy engineering products and manufactured goods. But the US and Germany soon captured these markets. The colonies in Asia and Africa began using their own industrial products. Tariff boundaries erected by the lucrative markets of America and Europe to protect domestic companies made British goods uncompetitive in the export market. When the American Depression began, Britain was hit by a crisis.. The government of James Ramsay MacDonald was caught in a difficult situation as it had no option but to raise taxes to balance the budget, but taxation at that juncture was politically dangerous because the people were already reeling under unemployment, and high prices. So the experiment of having a ‘National Government’ was tried out for the first time with representatives of all political parties included in it. while MacDonald remained Prime Minister. It won the elections in 1931 and carried out drastic measures to save the economy, even if it meant taking unpopular steps like increasing taxes and reducing wages. A preferential trading arrangement was worked out with Canada and Australia, which helped stimulate exports again. From 1934 onwards, the British economy once again began showing signs of recovery.
